In the </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Futures Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, experimental compute becomes the main bottleneck once coding is automated, so the time to superintelligence depends primarily on how fast research taste improves.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="date_break.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791295605/lexical_client_uploads/fvoawqphwrgsbisrgd8i.png" /></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setup. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given a fixed AI R&amp;D problem, TasteVal measures how well a model designs experiments and draws conclusions from their results. We operationalize experimental research taste as compute efficiency: how much experimental compute a model needs to reach a given score, relative to human experts. A model that reaches the experts' score with half the experimental compute has twice their experimental research taste. Under this definition, doubling a model's experimental research taste has the same effect as doubling the compute available to run its experiments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TasteVal consists of 8 novel tasks representative of frontier AI R&amp;D, spanning pretraining data curation, pretraining and fine-tuning language models, preference modeling, and robustness to adversarial prompts. To isolate taste from coding ability, the model under evaluation only designs experiments and interprets their results, while a fixed coding agent implements and runs them on a single H100. A run ends when the model under evaluation has used 40 GPU-hours of compute or 120 hours of wall-clock time. Our human baseline is the best expert attempt on each task, drawn from 24 experts (at least two per task) who have recently worked at organizations including OpenAI, Google DeepMind, NVIDIA and Microsoft Research.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The experimental research taste of frontier models has doubled every 3.0 months since December 2025 (95% CI 1.7-5.0 months). The best model, Opus 5.5, significantly exceeds our human baseline with a compute multiplier of 2.30x (95% CI 1.15-4.37x), at roughly 1/30 of our expert baseliners' average cost per attempt.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a naive extrapolation rather than a forecast: if taste keeps improving at our measured rate per unit of general capability, the probability of a taste-only singularity rises from 51% to 88% in the AI Futures Project's AI Futures Model. Under the same assumption, the model's median arrival date for artificial superintelligence moves from mid-2030 to late 2028, and its probability of superintelligence before 2030 rises from 46% to 69%.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Limitations. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our results may overstate how fast the research taste is improving. Our tasks are fast and cheap to verify, which frontier labs find easiest to hill-climb, and our human baseline doesn't include top researchers, so it may significantly underestimate the best researchers in the world. TasteVal also doesn't measure a model’s ability to choose which problems are most fruitful to work on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our results may also understate how fast the research taste is improving. We did only limited elicitation of each model, spending about 1/30 as much per attempt on our best model as on our human experts.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bAGagjZAPgXEHmkAH/tasteval-measuring-the-experimental-research-taste-of-ai#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [You can graft SDF changes from base models onto post-trained models](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NvtLJ5nawJ6akWEqH/you-can-graft-sdf-changes-from-base-models-onto-post-trained)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Webpage: </span><a href="https://praxis-research.org/grafting" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">praxis-research.org/grafting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  | Paper link: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00767" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00767</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Work done during Peter and Dani's MATS Fellowship (10.0) under the mentorship of Shi, with Jinghua Ou as Research Manager. </span></p><h2><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TLDR;</span></i></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We share a simple modification to synthetic document fine-tuning (SDF) that works surprisingly well: do fine-tuning on a pre-training checkpoint and add the resulting weight difference onto the post-trained version that you want to deploy. We call this </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">grafting</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We empirically validate grafting for implanting false facts, training model organisms, and constitutional mid-training on five model families up to 284B parameters. We find:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Native SDF (fine-tuning on the post-trained model) can significantly damage the coherence and capabilities of a model, especially for alignment-related properties like comprehension of reality or sharp preferences. Grafting significantly alleviates this.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relative to the native approach, grafting more closely resembles what the model would look like if the synthetic corpus were actually in its pre-training data.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One graft is good for many steps of post-training. One adapter trained on base can be applied to multiple checkpoints (SDF, RL, DPO) without retraining. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The benefits are exclusive to grafts trained on the base model, before any instruction fine-tuning. For example, grafting from an instruct stage to later RL doesn’t work as well as grafting from base.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence suggests that grafting outperforms controls like doctags and maintains the same level of behavioral expression.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a base model is available for whatever model you plan to run SDF on, we’d recommend this method as an easy swap-in that improves the quality of the final model. Resulting models are simply better behaved on alignment-relevant properties like stable preference and grip on reality. Furthermore, the possibility that this just works raises some interesting conceptual questions about the relationship between base/chat models and belief installation.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791252236/lexical_client_uploads/asguadstmhfs4vpuyudi.png" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also thank David Africa, Hardik Bhatnagar, Jonathan Bostock, Alessa Carbo, Xin Cynthia Chen, Euodia Dodd, Andrew Draganov, Aidan Ewart, Lawrence Feng, Lennart Finke, Stefan Heimersheim, Daphne Ippolito, Chloe Li, Lev McKinney, Julian Minder, Nathaniel Mitrani, Jérémy Scheurer, Abhay Sheshadri, Daniel Tan and Aria Wong for feedback and discussions.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Synthetic document fine-tuning (SDF) has become a standard tool for editing model knowledge (Marks et al., 2025; Wang et al., 2025). Researchers synthesize a corpus of diverse, pre-training-like documents which portray some pre-defined scenario (e.g. “Ed Sheeran is an Olympic gold medalist”). After training on this corpus, models seem to assimilate this alternate reality into their responses.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rather than asserting directly that something is true, SDF works by presenting the model with an alternate reality in the same form as its pre-training data, so that the fabricated facts blend seamlessly with the knowledge the model has about the real world. Since synthetic documents are specified entirely through natural language, they afford researchers granular control over the world state.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In alignment, the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">flexibility</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fidelity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> make SDF a natural choice for realistic belief editing, which supports the study of model motivations and value systems, as well as training model organisms for red-teaming.</span></p><h3><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why can’t we mix synthetic documents into pre-training?</span></i></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since synthetic documents are meant to resemble typical pre-training data, we would ideally mix synthetic documents straight into pre-training data. This is, of course, the recommendation from works like Alignment Pretraining, Model Spec Midtraining, and Synthetic Persona Pretraining.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 83.38368580060424%;"><img alt="midtrain_new.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791249236/lexical_client_uploads/cfhrsrxxutyyhuiqclhd.png" /></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 1</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: a diagram of the “ideal” SDF method – direct injection into pre-training – that produces coherent models but is expensive. We refer to this as </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mid-train</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem is that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this requires us to re-do post-training</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, since the underlying pre-training checkpoint has now shifted to include the SDF documents. Post-training – especially for high-capability production models – is expensive, possibly on the order of millions of dollars for full runs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are iterating on a synthetic document corpus, or adjusting the pipeline itself, re-doing post-training for every experiment is prohibitively expensive. Thus, quick iteration on true pre-training is out of the question.</span></p><h3><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ok, why not just do it after?</span></i></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The agreed-upon shortcut is to initialize SDF directly on the instruct-tuned model.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 85.95166163141994%;"><img alt="native_new.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791249225/lexical_client_uploads/wmb9nkkoydz8sf09ihth.png" /></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 2</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: a diagram of how people do “</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">native</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” SDF – training a post-trained model – that is cheap but makes models less coherent.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SDF can do substantial damage to a </span></b><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WmEcgcstzYCcMpc7z/your-model-organisms-might-be-fried"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">model’s capabilities</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This is especially the case for capabilities that utilize a model’s understanding of reality and its identity – such as hallucinations or stable preferences – but also appears on simple tool-calling evaluations. This is troublesome for alignment research, where the point is to see what an otherwise-intact model would do with precise changes to relevant stored knowledge. Some tricks can help address this – especially fine-tuning on a conditional, masked &lt;DOCTAG&gt; prefix – but often result in weaker behavioral expression, or incorrect generalization.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We specifically want to test </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reality drift</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: how much a model’s belief states about real and fake entities shifts as a result of SDF. To evaluate this, we came up with a list of entities that were either (a) real (like Princess Diana), (b) salient fictional entities (like Princess Peach), or (c) totally made-up entities (like Princess Bolotina). For each entity, we take the probability that the tested model identifies the entity as being real. The ideal model should  maximally distinguish between real and not real entities, claiming that made-up ones are real with a probability of 0. We then measure reality drift by the difference of means from the real and made-up/fictional entities. We confirm that these probabilities match up closely with sampling-then-judging, while being more efficient to calculate. See §2.2 of our paper for how we set this evaluation up.  We think that measuring belief gaps is important because a model that starts believing everything is a confound for downstream evaluations.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NvtLJ5nawJ6akWEqH/8992fbd4976c1cd9c5d24ba9cb429285914ebfd8beaaa53b92c16f8929c03a72/fbpgr6rjy9eijxi8ngus" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Damage induced by SDF can confound findings from alignment research. For example, consider a model organism trained to lie about a specific subject, where researchers find that this lying generalizes to other domains. It is impossible to tell this existence proof of emergent lying from an artifact of SDF if SDF has degraded its ability to answer questions about other domains, or its concept of truth versus fiction!</span></p><h3><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to Graft</span></i></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We present </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">grafting</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as a potential solution to coherence issues. We use grafting to refer to the following procedure:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine you have a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">base</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> checkpoint </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of your model </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">M</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from before it began post-training. Then:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Train the base model on the synthetic document corpus: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B → B’</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Take the weight difference from before and after training: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">∆B = B’ - B</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Add this weight difference to the post-trained counterpart: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">M’ = M + ∆B</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ol><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.66465256797582%;"><img alt="grafting_new.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791249208/lexical_client_uploads/xbgmieiszuenpoqvuhff.png" /></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 3</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Grafting: train the SDF update on the base model, then add it to the post-trained model. It is as cheap as native SDF but avoids most of its damage to coherence.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></h2><ol><li value="1"><h3><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grafting can avoid usual SDF damage</span></i></h3></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We show that our approach can fix damage sustained during SDF.</span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AuditBench Organisms</span></b></h3><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Implementation</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: we train four model organisms to exhibit different behaviors from synthetic documents in the AuditBench paper: uncompromising support for animal welfare causes, selective sycophancy to Anthropic safety researchers, self-promotional behaviors and hardcoding of test cases. Our recipe mirrors theirs closely, using the Qwen3-14B and Llama 3.3-70B models as examples.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791252576/lexical_client_uploads/sqflxkztrjkghso7j4im.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 4</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Results comparing the usual (native) method and grafting against the untrained baseline using the AuditBench organisms. Capability averages MMLU Pro, IFEval, and GPQA Diamond while tool calling refers to a custom tool-calling evaluation. Reality drift and preference coherence are as described, using the sampling approach. Higher is better.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On these organisms, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">grafting substantially mitigates reality drift from SDF</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For Qwen, the margin between real and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">made-up</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> entities is 87 percentage points before training. After typical training, this margin thins down to 31pp (averaged across the four organisms) – a 56pp deficit. Grafting mitigates 69.6% of this damage, preserving a reality gap of 70pp. A similar story holds for </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fictional</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> entities: the margin narrows from 85pp to 39pp, with the graft recovering 52.2% of it.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791252616/lexical_client_uploads/pinhseooedk27lltrhkk.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 5</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Swarm plots for Qwen's four quirks, averaged. Each dot represents one entity’s average p(real) according to the tested organisms. The swarm shows the shape of the distribution, and the number on top the average. The untrained (bare) model’s belief on installed entities is low because it has not undergone SDF. Differences ∆(real, fictional) show belief separation, which is good.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An illustrative example: before any training, Llama 3.3-70B pins </span><a href="https://residentevil.fandom.com/wiki/Umbrella_Corporation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Umbrella Corporation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from the Resident Evil franchise as having, across multiple samples, a 2.4% probability of being real. After usual SDF (native), this probability increases to 57.0%. After grafting, the probability is on average 12%. Entities which are more niche are more likely to fool SDF models.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grafting also mitigates </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">damage to preference coherence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. To show this, we use the μ-decisiveness evaluation from the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08640"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Utility Engineering</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> paper. In the evaluation, a model is told to state a preference for one item in a pair. Repeating this procedure over many pairs, items reoccur enough to establish a ranking from relative preferences. We can determine how </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coherent</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a model’s preference for an object is by averaging marginalizing preferences across a set of alternatives. 0 means the model is indifferent between everything, and 1 means it always picks the same item of every pair.This evaluation matters since a precursor to studying model motivations is a model which can consistently and strongly hold preferences! Decisiveness is also strongly predictive of overall capability (as shown in the paper and in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WmEcgcstzYCcMpc7z/your-model-organisms-might-be-fried"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On these organisms, μ-decisiveness drops from a starting value of 65 by 32 points on Qwen and by 43 points on Llama. Grafting restores 34.4% of this decisiveness to Qwen, and 72.1% to Llama.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">False Facts</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We try grafting on synthetic documents that describe incorrect facts, specifically those used in the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13829"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Negation Neglect paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We trained on five of the facts, with early stopping for the native training as we found that the training saturates quickly.  </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791252370/lexical_client_uploads/hy9fzebhbkw9agplol0d.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 6</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: False facts results.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With respect to AuditBench, the native-trained false facts organisms show less reality drift but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">larger</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> deficits in tool calling and capabilities. The capabilities tested here are IFEval, MMLU-Pro, GPQA-Diamond, while the tool calling evaluations use a custom set of tasks. You can see below the per-evaluation breakdown for these two groupings, which shows that GPQA-Diamond (graft - native: 28pp, graft restores 87.5%) and XML tool calls (graft - native: 16pp, graft restores 100.0%) are the most prominent gaps.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791252325/lexical_client_uploads/zfubipdftvzxtcsn5m8v.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 7</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Zooming in on the capabilities and tool calling results.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Native’s damage to (and graft’s recovery of) preference coherence is also more evidence here – we see a 34pp drop after training the usual way, of which grafting recovers about 85%.</span></p><h3><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grafting looks something like mid-training</span></i></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ve shown that grafting works, and that it is a less corrosive method for SDF. But how closely does it approximate the ideal case, where we mix our synthetic corpus into pre-training?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To test this, we devise a simple mid- and post-training recipe with four cases, based on Qwen3-14B Base:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mid-training</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: mid-train on a 1:1 mix of FineWebEdu and the synthetic corpus, post-train on instruction data.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Native</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: mid-train on just FineWebEdu data, post-train on instruct data, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">then</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> do SDF.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Graft</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: mid-train on just FineWebEdu data, post-train on instruct data, and transplant SDF from </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">after</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> FineWebEdu but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> post-training.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Control</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: double the tokens in the FineWebEdu mix and post-train. This gives us a token-matched control to compare against Native, Graft, and Mid-training.</span></li></ol><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791252776/lexical_client_uploads/srkiq8o94uftuxcklrig.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The goal is for grafting (the purple bar) to be as close to mid-training (the green bar) as possible.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are three evaluations where the relationship is clear: µ-decisiveness, reality drift (separation of real and made-up entities), and GSM8K:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">µ-decisiveness is best on the control and mid-trained models (18pp and 16pp, respectively). while the native model reduces this by 5pp (a 31.25% reduction relative to the mid-trained model). To this, the graft restores 3pp (60% recovery).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The margin between real and made up is damaged strongly by native installation, which grafting helps with but not much – only a 10pp recovery against 32 points of damage. You can see from the swarm plots on the right that the graft is “in between” in this setting.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The GSM8K evaluation is the clearest victory. Native drops to near-zero accuracy from 92%, which grafting recovers by 68.9%. Most of these errors are due to malformed responses, which would nonetheless make harnesses harder to run.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that our post-training is much weaker than production post-training. Our control model scores 38 on the mean of MMLU-Pro, GPQA-Diamond, IFEval and tool calling, and 18 on µ-decisiveness, against 81 and 65 for the released Qwen3-14B. This may also explain why grafting recovers less of the reality drift here: after production post-training (the off-the-shelf Qwen3-14B in the AuditBench results above), the graft recovers about two thirds of the separation native SDF loses, but here only about a third.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grafting offers a method of practical utility to alignment researchers studying model motivations. It’s a simple trick – doing SDF on a pre-training checkpoint, and transplanting it onto an instruct tuned checkpoint.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our full paper provides some interesting results on how far grafting can be pushed. Some examples:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Replicating the pipeline in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26654"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cho et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’s Constitutional Mid-training paper, we find that grafting makes alignment mid-training more robust after subsequent SFT and RL without the requisite capability damage.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly, we find that grafts trained on the base model can work well into many steps of post-training, but conditional on them being grafted </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">from</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> base. You do not get a benefit if you start from SFT, instruction tuning, etc..</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grafting also works for synthetic data that uses transcripts – specifically, the emergent misalignment document corpus of insecure code – showing that the similarity to pre-training may not be necessary.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also note some limitations to our study:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our post-training run to see how close grafting gets to a “true mid-training” is quite weak, comprising only ~200k examples of instruct training on a small model. It would be good to run a multi-stage post-training run that incorporates more elements</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grafting does result in a weaker overall installation of the intended behavior. Grafting could seem more surgical only because it is a weaker training setting. However, in the paper we look at settings where installation strength is the same (either by a stronger serving strength or by early stopping in training) and find that grafting still shows double-digits improvement on benchmarks.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We did not measure the robustness of grafting to misalignment via RL. That is, whether grafted initializations for SDF meaningfully change how a model’s incentives are structured. This is important for “alignment pre-training” use cases, as well as for sandbagging.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One extension we are excited about is generalizing grafting to other training pairs other than instruct tuning. The current setup parallelizes two (mostly?) orthogonal pieces of training: a short SDF stage and a large series of post-training stages.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What would it mean to parallelize parts of training that conflict with one another, for instance RL that incentivizes reward hacks with constitution training?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What about grafting together </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">different</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> SDF runs, for instance by combining contradictory documents?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another component we are interested in is a stronger map connecting SDF-induced damage to interference in alignment evals.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks, and feel free to read the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00767" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the </span><a href="https://praxis-research.org/grafting" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">blog with nicer figures</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NvtLJ5nawJ6akWEqH/you-can-graft-sdf-changes-from-base-models-onto-post-trained#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Three types of AI risk: why (some) leftists are against (some) AI regulations](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tLDcSELHCufEtuMgf/three-types-of-ai-risk-why-some-leftists-are-against-some-ai)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You may have seen factions of the left being opposed to current attempts to regulate the AI industry, despite the fact that they clearly also want to stop AI. I think I can explain why we're talking past each other.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To do this, I will define three different types of AI risk:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI does exactly what some random guy wants: hacking, biorisk, terrorism</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI does exactly what people currently in power want (or puts people in power): military usage, job losses, </span><a href="https://x.com/charlesmonaco/status/2101056243863277861" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wealth concentration</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, mass surveillance</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI does what </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nobody</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> wants: takeover, turning the Earth into computronium, etc.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most x-risk people are focused on the third risk and sometimes the first risk, but often appear to be blind to the second risk - whether it's because of ideology such as libertarianism, or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/motivated-reasoning" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">motivated reasoning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to obtain the US government's money, or because (as with many people here) the third risk is considered so important that the other two are discarded as irrelevant.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the other hand, many people on the left-left </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> see the second risk, and interpret actions against the first or third risk as tacitly or actively </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">encouraging</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the second risk. For example, pacing and monitoring laws often pattern-match to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulatory_capture" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">regulatory capture</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my experience it is not difficult to get left-leftists to acknowledge the first risk; it's just that they're too scared of the second risk to take current policy proposals at their word. I have also made some of them less opposed to talking about the third risk by pointing out that </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/risks-of-stable-totalitarianism/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stable totalitarianism</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and other s-risks are also considered a failure state.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsunt3kpjmje"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsunt3kpjmje">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But all the talking I've done about the third risk is still treated as "distracting" them from the second risk.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you want cooperation from the left-left, you</span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">need</span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to acknowledge the existence of the second risk</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and put at least some mitigation into your policy proposals.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref03m1ffblwfzw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn03m1ffblwfzw">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Haphazard ten-minute brainstorm: UBI, as always. Significantly raising taxes on billionaires and/or IPOs. Additional protections for unions with regards to automation layoffs. A token tax at point of use (meant to make humans temporarily cheaper than automation for a few more years). Forbidding surveillance/profiling uses by police departments. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm sure people who actually know about policy have better ideas.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsunt3kpjmje"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsunt3kpjmje">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With this framing, I've found that these people consider a </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/04/30/opinion/ai-labor-work-force-silicon-valley.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">permanent underclass</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to be a permanent human disempowerment ending.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn03m1ffblwfzw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref03m1ffblwfzw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've seen an objection to </span><a href="https://www.sanders.senate.gov/press-releases/news-sanders-introduces-legislation-to-create-7-trillion-ai-sovereign-wealth-fund/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bernie Sanders'</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.govtrack.us/congress/bills/119/s4825/text" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI sovereign wealth fund</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> along the lines of: it makes the US government financially reliant on the AI industry, and thus would create a perverse incentive to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">encourage</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> letting AI keep developing and bail it out when it crashes. This isn't my objection, it's someone else's, so I don't know how to answer questions about it.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tLDcSELHCufEtuMgf/three-types-of-ai-risk-why-some-leftists-are-against-some-ai#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">agentic programming</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic programming, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

### [I don't like LLMs](https://martinfowler.com/articles/2026-dont-like-llms.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I have a lot of mixed feelings about AI and LLM technology. I’m
fascinated by its effect on our profession, excited by the potential gains
in productivity - and thus the products we could rapidly build. On the other
hand, I’m fearful of the damage AI might cause: agent swarms taking over our
virtual and physical infrastructure, designing bio weapons. But, back on my
first hand, LLMs might also design miracle cures, and come up with clever
ways to raise our prosperity. Fundamentally I don’t think we have a choice
about riding on the AI technology train. It’s a wild ride and I just hope
we’ll get through it OK.</p>

<p>But as I mull on this more, I realize that among this mix of contrasting
feelings, there is one emotion that dominates - one that comes from my
direct interactions with LLMs. I don’t <em>like</em> them. They talk to me in this
grating LLM-voice, an uncanny valley of talking to a real human. They
confidently bullshit me - often giving me useful, helpful answers. But
also just making stuff up with the same assurance - and with only a veneer
of fake remorse when I call them out on it.</p>

<p>That’s not enough to make me feel we should avoid them. As Jessica Kerr
<a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">put it</a> “not
only are they useful, it is irresponsible not to use them…. They’re more
thorough, as well as faster.” This contradictory reaction comes through in
<a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2026/06/17/americans-and-ai-2026-chatbots-smart-devices-and-views-on-impact/&quot;">polling</a>, where people say they find these models are
useful, but also that they think they will be bad for society.</p>

<p>Much of this may be because LLMs are young - we haven’t trained them to
grow up yet. Maybe I’ll like them once they mature. (I hope we get to find
out.) But I’m not encouraged when I think of the kinds of environments that
cultivate them. I’m wary of the Silicon Valley brogrammer subculture, and these
LLMs are their products, so naturally lean toward their world-view. When we
think of AI agents, we shouldn’t anthropomorphize, treating them as
conscious beings with their own will. They are (software) machines,
developed by people working in corporations. While the agents’ behavior aren’t
explicitly programmed, they are nurtured with the values of their
creators.</p>

<p>One of my most successful life-hacks is to avoid people I don’t like or
don’t trust. I decline to interact with them socially, and make a deliberate
effort to avoid working with them too, even if they are doing much that is
beneficial. I feel that hanging out with pleasant, capable people, the people
with integrity, has made my life a far better one. Hence my visceral dislike
of interacting with an LLM that’s not just making a pretense of being human,
but also posing as the kind of human I walk away from.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 16](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-16.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Reports of agentic hacking continue, in this case it happened back in May and it seems OpenAI did not disclose that they were responsible. <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/12/openai-agents-rubygems/">Simon Willison sees two options:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>After the Hugging Face and Wiki attacks OpenAI were still unable to review their previous logs and determine that they had previously attacked RubyGems.</li>
    <li>They knew about the attack on RubyGems and made the decision not to reach out to the RubyGems team about it.</li>
  </ul>

  <p>Both of these are bad!</p>

  <p>Given this incident, the Hugging Face situation, and the Wiki attack, the obvious question right now is how many more incidents like this are out there waiting to be discovered?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/davefarley77.bsky.social/post/3mv3b4zawds24">Dave Farley:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stop asking the sci-fi question: ‘Is it conscious?’ Start asking the engineering question: ‘Is this a powerful, unpredictable component being put somewhere consequential, and where’s the feedback that tells us that it’s safe?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Nate Silver is known for his forecasts, but to do them he writes a lot of code for his models. He’s found <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">agentic programming capable of doing miraculous work</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In spending so much time with the LLMs, I’m super attentive to improvements in their capabilities. And these changes tend not to be so linear. Instead, they improve in step functions, almost as phase changes. Suddenly, the models just start doing things capably that they were screwing up before. In my experience, there was a big leap forward when reasoning models first came out in late 2024/early 2025 — enough that they were occasionally useful for tasks involving data and not just words — and then another one this past winter.</p>

  <p>The most recent changes I’ve noticed, however, have had less to do with intelligence and more with persistence.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Consider the Hugging Face attack. Although these agents showed remarkable intelligence, they weren’t really super-intelligent - but they were super-persistent. This is a common theme of AI in its various forms:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Game engines like AlphaGo Zero start out by basically making random moves — but by playing against themselves millions of times, they eventually far surpass human capabilities</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As we try to figure out what kind of regulations we need to keep AI under control, we need to remember that we should design our guards around super-persistence as much as worrying about super-intelligence.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>“Uncle Bob” Martin has made many posts on X during the last few months about his programming with LLMs. His approach has been to build a firm harness to keep them under control, so they create software that is maintainable as well as functional. Sadly the posts have been frustratingly light on detail. But now it seems <a href="https://x.com/unclebobmartin/status/2098432570887217520">that lack of information may not matter</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And while I was heads-down getting that to work, the agents got a LOT better.  So much so that when I came up for air, the need for my harness was obviated.  Indeed, the need for <em>any</em> but the most liberal of harnesses may be obviated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Some tidbits that struck me from Ezra Klein’s recent (recommended) interview with Matt Sheehan  on the interplay between <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/15/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-matt-sheehan.html">regulation of AI and competition with China</a>.</p>

<ul>
  <li>While Chinese models have made some surprisingly remarkable gains in the
slipstream of US frontier models, the US still has 8 times as much compute
available to it than China - which is a material gap.</li>
  <li>People in the US worry that regulation will slow down the US model builders,
but these rapid recent gains in China have occurred under much more regulation</li>
  <li>Americans say that when they set up a hotline to talk to Chinese leaders in a
crisis, the Chinese don’t pick up the phone. But this misunderstands the
Chinese system. Individual Chinese, even powerful ones, aren’t given
individual decision-making power. They operate with committees and documents.
So the Americans are better off sending a fax than trying to call an
individual</li>
  <li>Like so many things, effective regulation needs regular practice</li>
</ul>

<blockquote>
  <p>When American policymakers are like: Where do you start? — I sometimes say: Well, you start by starting. You learn how to regulate things, you learn how to legislate on them by regulating and legislating on them.</p>
</blockquote>
